#10746. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.

#10743. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

“Tech doesn’t move forward by clinging to outdated systems, and neither does fashion,” Reiman said. “PETA is calling on everyone to embrace vegan leathers made from cactus, cork, apples, and other plant materials and leave animals out of their wardrobes.”

The jacket is made using Oleatex, a leather alternative derived from olive harvesting waste. It features an organic cotton lining and an exterior consisting of 95% Biotex and 5% polyurethane.

PETA has also asked Huang to donate his old leather jacket to its planned Museum of Animal-Sourced Atrocities, offering the billionaire the prospect of a tax deduction in return.

#10738. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10734. Samsung might copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island soon, but it actually looks good [Video]

A new leak suggests that Samsung is preparing to copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island feature in the not-too-distant future, to an extent that not many other Android brands have even done thus far.

#10732. How to Improve Email Deliverability Rates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You wrote a good email. You hit send. Then half your list never saw it, because it went straight to spam.

That is a deliverability problem, and it costs more than most teams think. Every email that misses the inbox is a lead you paid for and never reached.

The good part is that deliverability is not luck. Mailbox providers like Gmail, Yahoo and Outlook judge you on a short list of things: who you are, who you send to, and how people react. Fix those and your inbox placement goes up.

This guide covers 10 email deliverability best practices in the order you should fix them, starting with SPF and ending with sender reputation.

Email deliverability is the share of your emails that land in the inbox instead of the spam folder or nowhere at all.

People often mix it up with delivery rate. They are not the same thing.

You can have a 99% delivery rate and still have most of your emails sitting in spam. That is why your email tool can say "delivered" while your open rate keeps dropping.

The big mailbox providers stopped treating authentication as optional.

Microsoft followed on May 5, 2025. If you send more than 5,000 emails a day to Outlook.com, Hotmail or Live addresses, you need all three records in place or your mail goes to junk.

Send less than 5,000 a day? Follow the rules anyway. The same checks decide inbox placement for small senders too, just without the public warning.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework) is a TXT record in your DNS. It lists the servers allowed to send email from your domain.

When your email arrives, the receiving server checks that list. If the sending server is not on it, the email looks suspicious.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) adds a digital signature to each email. The receiving server uses a public key in your DNS to check two things: the email came from you, and nobody changed it on the way.

Your email provider gives you the DKIM record. You paste it into your DNS and switch signing on.

DKIM also survives forwarding, which SPF does not. If you can only get one of the two perfect, get DKIM right.

DMARC ties SPF and DKIM together. It tells mailbox providers what to do when an email fails both checks, and it sends you reports on who is sending mail under your domain name.

p=none means "take no action, just send me reports." It is the minimum Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft accept from bulk senders.

Don't stop there. Read the reports for a few weeks, fix any real sender that is failing, then move up:

One more thing: DMARC needs alignment. The domain in your From address has to match the domain used in SPF or DKIM. Plenty of senders have all three records and still fail DMARC because their email tool signs with its own domain.

If you'd rather not handle this in-house, you can also hire a B2B cold email marketing agency such as IntentSignal, Martal or Belkins. A good agency will set up the domains, mailboxes, warm-up and SPF, DKIM and DMARC records for you, and keep an eye on deliverability while the campaigns run.

A new domain has no history. If it sends 10,000 emails on day one, it looks exactly like a spammer who just bought a domain.

Warm-up means starting small and growing a little each day, so mailbox providers can watch how people react to you.

Send to your most active contacts first. Their opens and replies are what build trust early. If bounces or complaints jump, hold at that volume until they settle.

And if you run cold outreach, do it from a separate domain or subdomain. That way a bad campaign can't hurt the domain your invoices and password resets come from.

A bigger list is not a better list. Dead addresses and people who stopped caring pull your numbers down.

Never buy a list. Bought lists are full of spam traps, which are addresses that exist only to catch senders who email people without permission. Hitting a few of them can get your domain blocklisted.

Yes, you lose some sign-ups. What you keep is real people with working inboxes who actually want your emails. That means fewer bounces, fewer complaints and better opens from day one.

It also keeps bots and fake addresses out of your forms, which matters a lot if your sign-up form is public.

Most email platforms add these for you. Check that yours does.

These two numbers tell you the most about where you stand.

The complaint figures come from Gmail's own rules. The bounce figures are the working limits most email platforms use.

A 0.3% complaint rate is only 3 complaints per 1,000 emails. It does not take much to cross that line.

If complaints are climbing, ask three questions:

Most complaint problems come from one of those three, not from the email itself.

Spam filters got smarter. A single word like "free" won't sink you anymore. Patterns will.

Replies help a lot. When someone replies, the mailbox provider sees a real conversation. Ask a question in your welcome email and you'll get some.

Sender reputation is the score mailbox providers give your domain and IP. Everything above feeds into it. A good score means inbox. A bad one means spam, no matter how good the email is.

You can't see the exact score, but these free tools get you close:

Check them weekly, and right after any big campaign.

A few habits protect your reputation over time:

Aim for 95% or higher inbox placement. Below 90% means something needs fixing, usually authentication or list quality.

The common causes are missing or broken SPF, DKIM or DMARC records, a high complaint rate, an old list with lots of dead addresses, or a new domain that sent too much too soon. Check authentication first because it is the fastest to fix.

DNS fixes like SPF and DKIM take effect within a day or two. Reputation is slower. Expect two to six weeks of steady, clean sending before inbox placement recovers.

Yes. The 5,000 mark is where Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft start enforcing openly. Small senders are still judged on the same signals, and Gmail asks every sender for at least SPF or DKIM.

Only if you send enough to keep it warm, roughly 100,000 emails a month or more. Below that, a good shared IP from a trusted provider usually performs better.

Sender reputation covers both your IP address and your domain. Domain reputation is the part tied to your domain name, and it follows you even if you switch email providers. Gmail leans on domain reputation more than IP.

If you only have an hour this week, spend it on SPF, DKIM and DMARC. Nothing else works until those pass.

After that, clean your list and keep an eye on complaints. Send emails people asked for, to people who still want them, from a domain that proves who it is. Do that for a few weeks and your emails will land in the inbox again.

#10731. 103-year-old grandmother competing in Tekken 8 tournament but doesn’t actually want to win

A 103-year-old gaming grandmother is taking part in a Tekken tournament, with one of her closest rivals also being 95 years old.

Even though gaming, and especially esports, are seen as a hobby for the younger generation, there are plenty of older folks who get involved as well. Those who grew up on the likes of the PlayStation One, Atari 2600, NES, and even the Sega Genesis still want to be involved.

Over in Japan, the Care Esports Association runs competitive gaming events aimed at senior citizens throughout the year, with an annual event taking place in December around Tekken 8.

Typically, competitors are aged between 70 and 90, but this year, they’ve cracked the 100-year-old mark, with a 103-year-old grandmother taking part.

As per Denfaminico, 103-year-old Toshiko Morishita will play in this year’s event, which is also looking to set a Guinness World Record for the “highest average age of esports tournament participants.”

According to the outlet, the 103-year-old knows that her age might be a barrier to success, but said that even losing would still be “enjoyable” for her.

She “enjoys the game” without “being solely focused on winning or losing,” but has been putting in practice to prepare for the event.

As noted, competitors at the event typically range from 70 to 90 years old, with last year’s winner – Hisako Sakai – clocking in at 92 years old.

Sakai fought her way to first place using Claudio Serafino and defeated 95-year-old Sadayuki Kato in the semifinals.

The average age at last year’s event was just under 87, so having a 103-year-old competing will drag that up – hopefully, for them, into Guinness World Records territory.

#10728. Galaxy Z Fold 8 features: What you can do on a foldable

Folding phones have come in numerous variations over the years as the concept has evolved. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the latest evolution that introduces a form factor that fits every user. Its unique design makes it a foldable most people can actually use – which makes it a perfect gift. You might be surprised by what it can do.

It’s in the name. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 folds and unfolds like other foldable devices – that much you know. But what the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s form factor provides is something different to what most might be familiar with.

Instead of a tall device that unfolds into a near-uniform square display, you get a wider screen more like a tablet. That makes it perfect for watching videos or movies. Content that naturally works well on TVs or your computer will look great on the Galaxy Z Fold 8.

What about content that’s best viewed in portrait mode? Because it opens up to a tablet-like display, you can turn the Fold 8 to watch YouTube Shorts or Instagram Reels without a problem, so if you or someone you know likes to watch content on a bigger display, the Z Fold 8 makes that much easier. It really doesn’t matter what content you come across – the wider inner display gives you that large screen feeling.

Like a well-designed tool, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 has more purposes than the tasks best suited for the inner display. That shorter, wider profile makes the outer display smaller, but not as thin as something on other foldables, like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra.

In reality, the aspect ratio is similar enough across both the inner and outer screens that most content scales well on the shorter front panel. Social apps like X and Instagram look great because videos and images are able to fill the screen, rather than squeeze. Samsung’s One UI software also supports app optimization across both screens, although the experience may vary by app.

Saying the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s outer display is “minimal” isn’t a dig, either. Apps can be refreshing to use when you don’t need to open them on a large scale. Take Messages, for instance. It’s not a complex app, and there isn’t a ton to see – just the last couple of messages exchanged. The same goes for plenty of apps available on the Google Play Store.

And guess what? If you don’t like using the app on a smaller display, just unfold the phone. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is such a versatile phone, whether it’s a gift during the holidays or one for yourself.

There are instances where you need the large real estate. One UI 9 gives the Galaxy Z Fold 8 the option to open and run multiple apps on your screen. There are several ways you can do it, too.

The main approach is to split two apps on the wide inner display. But if you want to see more from one app, you can simply drag the split over in one direction. One UI 9 leaves room to add one more app to that split screen, giving you a three-way Split View. All it takes is dragging an app from the taskbar and choosing the spot.

If you truly need a fourth app open, you have the option to open another in a floating window. Having four apps open at once is definitely a niche instance, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8 handles it like it’s nothing. You can have YouTube up in one corner while you check Messages for gift ideas and shop through the Amazon app. It might be helpful to keep a list of what you got and for whom in a floating Sheets window.

The point is not only that it’s technically possible, but it’s usable. So often devices let users open apps in split screen, but are in no way comfortable enough for the task. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is perfect for it, and the experience actually makes you more productive, not less.

Most phones don’t let you get the best angle without any sort of third-party accessory. The Galaxy Z Fold 8, though, acts like its own tripod in a way.

Let’s say you’re taking photos, and you need to get a shot of yourself, but no one is around to take the photo for you. The Galaxy Z Fold 8’s hinge lets it prop itself up, so you can do things like set a timer and run to strike a pose.

This comes in handy for videos, too. Think of how many times you wanted to get a good angle on a plane or somewhere where there’s this flat surface just asking for a tiny tripod or kickstand. Well, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is its own kickstand and can be positioned at just the right angle.

You can get the Galaxy Z Fold 8 from your favorite carriers and retailers, but Best Buy is one of the leading places to find deals during the holiday season. You’ll find hefty discounts, higher trade-in values for your old devices, and more.

#10726. NASA’s new AI space chip could let spacecraft think for themselves

NASA is developing a powerful new computer chip designed to dramatically increase the intelligence and performance of future spacecraft. Through a commercial partnership, the project is creating advanced processing technology capable of helping spacecraft operate more independently during missions far from Earth.

NASA's High Performance Spaceflight Computing project is focused on boosting the computing capabilities of spacecraft used in space exploration. Current missions rely on older processors because they are durable enough to survive the extreme conditions of space. While those chips are dependable, they lack the performance needed for more advanced missions.

The agency says newer and far more capable processors are essential for future autonomous spacecraft, faster onboard scientific analysis, and supporting astronauts during missions to the Moon and Mars.

"Building on the legacy of previous space processors, this new multicore system is fault-tolerant, flexible, and extremely high-performing," said Eugene Schwanbeck, program element manager in NASA's Game Changing Development program at the agency's Langley Research Center, in Hampton, Virginia. "NASA's commitment to advancing spaceflight computing is a triumph of technical achievement and collaboration."

Radiation Hardened Processor Faces Extreme Testing

At the center of the project is a new radiation-hardened processor built to deliver up to 100 times the computing power of today's spaceflight computers while surviving the harsh environment of space. Engineers at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California are running a wide range of tests designed to simulate those conditions.

"We are putting these new chips through the wringer by carrying out radiation, thermal, and shock tests while also evaluating their performance through a rigorous functional test campaign," said Jim Butler, High Performance Space Computing project manager at JPL.

To qualify for spaceflight, the processor must withstand intense electromagnetic radiation and dramatic temperature fluctuations that can damage electronics. High-energy particles from the Sun and deep space can also trigger computer errors that force spacecraft into "safe mode," temporarily shutting down nonessential systems until engineers resolve the issue.

NASA is also testing how the chip handles the challenges of planetary landings.

"To simulate real-world performance, we are using high-fidelity landing scenarios from real NASA missions that would typically require power-intensive hardware to process huge volumes of landing-sensor data," said Butler. "This is an exciting time for us to be working on hardware that will enable NASA's next giant leaps."

Testing at JPL began in February and is expected to continue for several months. Early results have been highly encouraging. According to NASA, the processor is functioning as intended and has shown performance levels roughly 500 times greater than the radiation-hardened chips currently used in spacecraft.

The team also marked the beginning of testing with a symbolic moment by sending an email titled "Hello Universe," referencing the famous introductory messages used during the early days of computer programming.

AI Powered Spacecraft and Deep Space Missions

The processor is being developed jointly by JPL and Microchip Technology Inc., based in Chandler, Arizona. The company is working with NASA through a commercial partnership, and sample chips have already been shared with defense and commercial aerospace partners.

The technology is expected to play a major role in the future of autonomous spacecraft. With onboard artificial intelligence, spacecraft could respond to unexpected situations in real time when communication delays make human control impractical. The chip could also help deep space missions process, store, and transmit massive amounts of scientific data back to Earth more efficiently.

NASA says the processor may eventually support crewed missions to the Moon and Mars as well.

Small Processor With Massive Computing Power

The device is known as a system-on-a-chip (or SoC), meaning it combines the essential components of a computer into a single compact unit. The processor includes central processing units, computational offloads, advanced networking systems, memory, and input/output interfaces.

SoCs are widely used in smartphones and tablets because they are compact and energy efficient. However, NASA's version is designed to survive for years in deep space, potentially traveling millions (or even billions) of miles from Earth without maintenance or repairs.

Once the processor is certified for use in space, NASA plans to integrate it into a wide variety of missions, including Earth orbiters, planetary rovers, deep space probes, and crewed habitats.

The technology could also have benefits on Earth. Microchip plans to adapt the processor for industries such as aviation and automotive manufacturing.

The project is managed by the Space Technology Mission Directorate's Game Changing Development (GCD) program at NASA Langley. The GCD program and JPL, which is managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, oversaw the development process from mission planning and industry studies through final delivery.

NASA JPL selected Microchip as a partner in 2022, and the company funded its own research and development work on the processor.

Materials provided by NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10725. Japan expands AI piracy hunt after finding pirated manga on 94% of flagged sites

Japan is expanding its AI crackdown on manga and anime piracy overseas after testing a system that can find websites hosting unauthorized copies.

On November 24, The Yomiuri Shimbun reported that Japan’s Agency for Cultural Affairs found pirated manga on 94% of the 1,935 websites its AI flagged for suspected manga piracy. Of the 1,564 sites flagged for suspected anime piracy, 89% hosted unauthorized copies.

The domestic trial also extended to X, where 84% of the 1,168 posts flagged by the AI were found to infringe copyright. These findings come nearly a year after Japan set aside ¥100 million to develop the system, with plans to expand its search to overseas piracy sites.

For its overseas tests, the agency trained the AI to recognize domain names commonly associated with piracy sites and could compile the results as early as March 2027.

The government was already exploring how AI could help enforce copyright when Dexerto covered the plans last year, and the agency is now considering whether the system could automatically submit takedown requests.

Japan’s latest anti-piracy push follows an April 3 discussion in the country’s House of Representatives, where executives from some of Japan’s biggest animation studios warned that piracy remains one of the biggest obstacles to anime’s growth overseas.

Among them was Toei Animation, the studio behind One Piece and Dragon Ball, which called for stronger government action on anime piracy.

According to data published by Japan’s Ministry of Economy, Trade, and Industry, piracy losses from digital content rose from two trillion yen in 2022 to 5.7 trillion yen in 2025, increasing from roughly $13.3 billion to around $38 billion USD.

In a bid to curb those losses, Japan’s Agency for Cultural Affairs has also expanded its AI piracy tests to target overseas sites hosting unauthorized copies of manga and anime, with plans to compile its findings as early as March 2027.

#10721. How to Use IP Geolocation to Stop Bots and Fraud (Without Hurting UX)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Several methods can help you deal with online threats and strengthen security. All are effective, but each has a trade-off. For example, the harder you make it for bots to affect your website’s performance, the more likely you are to frustrate real customers.

Those of you who browse online daily know that credential stuffing, fake signups & payment fraud keep growing. To prevent fraud, businesses install CAPTCHAs & hard blocks that treat bots and legitimate users the same, diverting them to your competitors.

IP geolocation maps an IP address to a physical location & network details, letting you spot suspicious traffic before it reaches sensitive actions. Used well, it’s an invisible, low-friction risk signal. In this guide, you will learn how to flag high-risk activity and apply soft geo-fencing with step-up authentication.

Today, an IP lookup returns far more than a country & city. It can also reveal several other parameters, like

Each of these fields acts as a clue telling who is really behind a request.

This data powers anomaly detection. You can start by building a baseline for normal behavior for each user with aspects like

Then, you can watch for deviations from that pattern. An attacker won’t look like the account’s real owner, so you'll catch them.

Geolocation makes several common attack patterns easy to spot:

The first is traffic bursts from data center ASNs. These are bots, as real customers don’t browse from cloud servers

Many accounts created from one IP or subnet. This is a clear sign of fake signups

Impossible travel, such as logins from two distant locations within minutes

Timezone vs. IP mismatch. A device’s clock disagrees with its apparent location.

Note: Geolocation is just a signal, not a verdict. VPNs, travel, & mobile carriers can all create false alarms, so it works best when combined with other signals. That’s exactly why a proportional, soft response beats a hard block.

In short, IP geolocation turns a bare IP address into context, helping you separate real users from automated or fraudulent traffic.

Not every IP geolocation anomaly deserves the same response. The simplest way to stay consistent is to define a clear risk-signal framework.

That can be a short list of checks. Each check can have a weight that reflects how strongly it suggests fraud.

With risk scoring, you can turn these checks into a decision. You just have to assign each signal a numeric weight.

For example, medium can be 10 & 30 can be high.

Now, you need to add up every signal that comes up & map the total to thresholds like

A single medium signal passes quietly, but several can trigger action.

If you treat this as an automatic block, it would punish legitimate customers. Instead, let it raise the score & you take action only if it stacks with other signals, like a hosting IP or a new device.

Now that you know how to quantify the risk, here is how you can protect yourself without damaging UX.

Before soft, let us learn about hard blocking. This is where you completely deny or permanently ban traffic from specific geographic regions.

Now, hard blocking by country may look effective, but it can backfire quickly. Here is how.

In the worst case, determined bot operators simply rotate to IPs in allowed regions, so the only people you stop are your real customers.

This is where the balance between security and user experience becomes critical. As the digital strategist and Founder at LawRank, Mariano Rodriguez emphasizes, "Your website's security should be a silent guardian, not a roadblock. If your fraud prevention creates friction, you aren't just stopping bots—you're turning away real clients. True digital trust is built when backend protections are seamless and invisible to the legitimate user."

Now, with soft geo-fencing, you can respond to risk proportionally instead of making a simple allow-or-deny call. Instead of totally banning the users, you adjust the level of friction to match the risk score from the previous section:

**Note: **For soft geo-fencing, passkeys are safest because they are phishing-resistant. Only a legitimate user can take the biometric tap and pass.

Before we start, let’s understand Express.js middleware. An Express.js middleware for Geolocation Risk Scores is a custom or 3rd party function that intercepts the incoming HTTP requests, extracts the client's IP address, fetches a security risk profile based on their geographic location & attaches that data to the request object (req) before it reaches your route handlers.

Here is how you can use it for fraud prevention and bot mitigation:

Note: You can make it stronger by adding velocity checks (too many attempts from one IP or account) & device fingerprints.

#10716. Runaway horse visits McDonald’s before leading police on neighborhood chase

A runaway horse in Virginia made an unexpected trip to McDonald’s before leading police on a chase through a residential neighborhood.

On November 17, police in Colonial Heights were called to a McDonald’s after a horse named Rolo was spotted wandering along a busy road.

The horse had escaped from Hickory Haven Equestrian in South Chesterfield after managing to roll underneath an electric fence.

Speaking to 12 On Your Side, owner Ashley Moyer explained that she believes the restaurant’s lights and the presence of people may have attracted Rolo.

“McDonald’s is a pretty straight shot,” she said.

Moyer also revealed that this wasn’t the first time the horse had escaped from the equestrian center, joking that “Rolo is a repeat offender.”

Two Virginia State Police troopers who were heading home from work encountered the horse while local officers were already in pursuit, joining efforts to bring the animal to safety.

Dashcam footage released by Virginia State Police shows officers following Rolo as he makes his way through the area before eventually being cornered at Shepard Stadium.

Officers used lassos to secure the horse, with one officer helping to keep him calm until he could be reunited with his owner.

“Using a little teamwork, some lassos, and the power of friendship, everyone worked together to safely corner and wrangle our four-legged runaway away from traffic,” Colonial Heights Animal Control said in a Facebook post.

Rolo’s owner was alerted to his escape after receiving several phone calls in the middle of the night, followed by a police officer knocking on her door.

#10715. Forget electrons, this breakthrough uses light-matter particles to power AI

Eighty years after the creation of ENIAC, the world's first general-purpose electronic computer, researchers at the University of Pennsylvania are exploring a new way to power the future of computing. Instead of relying entirely on electrons, which have formed the backbone of computers since the 1940s, scientists are now turning to light.

ENIAC, developed by Penn researchers J. Presper Eckert and John Mauchly, helped launch the modern computing era by using streams of electrons to solve complex mathematical problems. That same electronic approach still powers today's computers, smartphones, and AI systems. But as artificial intelligence grows more demanding, the limits of electron-based hardware are becoming harder to ignore.

Electrons carry an electrical charge, which creates several challenges inside modern computer chips. As they move through materials, they generate heat and face resistance that wastes energy. Those problems become even more difficult as chips grow more complex and process enormous amounts of data for AI applications.

Researchers led by Penn physicist Bo Zhen in the School of Arts & Sciences believe photons, the particles that make up light, could help solve some of these issues.

"Because they are charge-neutral and have zero rest mass, photons can carry information quickly over long distances with minimal loss, dominating communications technology," explains Li He, co-first author of a paper published in Physical Review Letters and a former postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab. "But that neutrality means they barely interact with their environment, making them bad at the sort of signal-switching logic that computers depend on."

In other words, light is excellent for carrying information quickly and efficiently, but it struggles with the switching operations needed for computing.

Combining Light and Matter for AI Computing

To overcome that problem, Zhen's team developed a special quasiparticle called an exciton-polariton. The particle forms when photons are strongly linked with electrons inside an atomically thin semiconductor material. This combination allows light to interact much more effectively, making it capable of performing the signal switching required for computing tasks.

The breakthrough could be especially important for artificial intelligence systems, which consume enormous amounts of power.

Many experimental photonic AI chips already use light to handle certain calculations at high speed. However, when these systems need to perform nonlinear activation steps, such as decision-making operations, they usually must convert light signals back into electronic ones. That conversion slows the process and increases energy use, reducing the benefits of photonic computing.

Using exciton-polaritons, the Penn researchers demonstrated all-light switching while using only about 4 quadrillionths of a joule of energy. That amount is extraordinarily small, far below the energy needed to briefly power a tiny LED light.

Toward Faster and More Efficient AI Chips

If the technology can be successfully scaled, it could lead to photonic chips capable of processing information directly from cameras without repeated conversions between light and electricity. The approach could also lower the massive energy demands of large AI systems and potentially support basic quantum computing functions on future chips.

Bo Zhen is the Jin K. Lee Presidential Associate Professor in the Department of Physics and Astronomy in the School of Arts & Sciences at the University of Pennsylvania.

Li He was a postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab in Penn Arts & Sciences. He is currently an assistant professor at Montana State University.

Additional authors on the study include Zhi Wang and Bumho Kim from the University of Pennsylvania's School of Arts & Sciences.

The research was supported by the US Office of Naval Research (N00014-20-1-2325 and N00014-21-1-2703) and the Sloan Foundation.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10712. The Google Home app is better than Nest alone ever was, mostly

The Nest app is officially dead, but it’s worth taking a step back and remembering that, while there are some rough edges, the Google Home app is a better smart home experience than Nest alone ever could have been.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The Nest app has been on life support for years now. Google has been migrating legacy products into the Home app for a while and the last few puzzle pieces came in Nest Protect support, the death of the earliest Nest Thermostats, and other assorted adjustments.

Realistically, even long-time Nest users have had no reason to continue using the app over Google Home.

Having been personally, deeply invested in Google’s vision for the smart home over the past decade, I’ll say that the Google Home app we have today is better than the Nest app ever was or ever could be. A huge part of that comes from Google Home’s ability to pull together devices from third-party ecosystems. Being able to control your entire smart home from a single app is how the smart home always should have been, and between Google’s work and the Matter standard, we’re finally able to enjoy that pretty seamlessly for the most part. Automations, Google’s actually-useful AI features, and more only sweeten the deal. I’m also a big fan of what the Home app has become from a UX perspective. While there are always rough edges and bugs to clean up, the customizable homepage and well-organized sections make for a good – not perfect, but good – experience for the end user.

That said, there is one thing that the Nest app’s limited scope benefitted from, and that’s a focus on making your own product great.

Google’s move to the Home app for Nest devices has been a 5-year run of trying to clean things up, improve on the app, and basically bring back the way things were in the Nest app as far as the first-party cameras and thermostats are concerned. It’s taken ages, but we’re finally up to speed, mostly. I still hate that you have to reset Nest Cams to change Wi-Fi when the Nest app just let you change it, and the Nest app’s settings were much less overwhelming, but I’d say the pros far outweigh the cons at this point.

The Nest app was good at its job, but the Google Home app is a worthy successor, finally, and now the Nest app can finally rest. I mostly think the same applies to Gemini vs Google Assistant, with the newer assistant a better overall experience, but it’s easier to poke holes in that argument.

#10709. 5 Best AI Anime Video Generators in 2026, and Are AI Anime Videos Worth Making?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI video generators can now turn a prompt or a character image into a few seconds of animation, and anime is one of the most popular styles people ask for. But anime is also one of the hardest styles to get right: flat colors, thin linework and stylized motion expose the weaknesses of video models quickly. For developers and designers, the useful question is not which tool is “best”, but what each one actually offers, what it cannot do yet, and how to fit it into a real production workflow.

This article compares five generators on what their own pages state, covers the problems they all share, asks whether AI anime videos are worth making, and then explains how the models work and how designers and developers can build a practical workflow, including ffmpeg and API-integration examples. “Best” here depends on the job: the tools are listed in no ranking order, each is linked once to its official site for reference, and the comparison is based on public vendor pages reviewed in November 2026. Features, models and pricing change often, so verify current details before relying on any of them.

We reviewed each vendor’s own site and compared what it states on the same points. This is a documentation-based comparison, not a lab benchmark. Where a page does not state something, we say so instead of guessing.

AI anime video generator by Pollo AI sits within a general AI video platform. On its main site, Pollo describes generating video from text prompts or uploaded images and editing existing videos, such as adding, changing, removing or restyling content. It also offers AI image generation, so a character image and the animation can be made in one place.

Strengths for anime work: text-to-video and image-to-video in the same tool, plus video editing and restyling, which suits experimenting with looks.

Limitations and gaps: the pages we could read do not name the underlying models, list credit amounts or pricing, or describe anime-specific controls such as reference characters or first and last frame inputs. You will need to test consistency yourself.

Fits: creators who want image, video and editing tools in one platform and are comfortable testing results before committing.

Vidu lists text-to-video, image-to-video and reference-to-video modes. For image-to-video you can set first and last frames. Reference-to-video lets you upload several images of a character, object or scene, and saved references can be reused through a My References feature. The site highlights 2D animation and diverse artistic styles, mentions 1080p output and offers an API platform aimed at enterprise use.

Strengths for anime work: the clearest set of consistency tools among the five pages we reviewed, with first and last frames, multi-image references and reusable saved characters.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not specify clip durations beyond examples, detail its anime-specific templates, or list prices. Free credits are mentioned without an amount.

Fits: sequences built around a recurring character, and teams that want an API.

Kling AI presents Kling 4.0 for video, described as offering stable dynamic motion and an audio-visual experience, alongside an image model, a Motion Control tool and a node-based Canvas editor for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video. It also lists MCP and CLI support for batch generation and automated production, and an API platform with documentation.

Strengths for anime work: motion-focused tooling and a workflow environment for storyboards and multi-step production, plus automation options for developers.

Limitations and gaps: the page we reviewed does not detail image-to-video specifics, first and last frame controls, camera control, durations, video resolution or pricing, and it gives no anime-specific guidance.

Fits: teams that value motion control and scripted or batch pipelines.

Hailuo AI, from MiniMax, describes creating AI videos and images from text or photos. Its page shows a reference field and an Omni Reference option, a banner for a model that mentions multimodal generation and editing, and many photo-based templates such as photo-to-motion, transformation and style transfer.

Strengths for anime work: an accessible, template-driven way to animate a still image and try stylized effects quickly.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not explain how reference works, or state camera control, durations, resolution, API availability or pricing. Template-driven results may be harder to control precisely than prompt- and frame-driven workflows.

Fits: quick experiments and social clips made from a single image.

Dreamina describes itself as an all-in-one creative platform for images, videos and talking avatars. For video it lists text-to-video, image-to-video, sketch-to-video, cartoon video generation and reference-to-video, using several Seedance models, along with editing examples such as green-screen replacement. It also offers image generation and upscaling.

Strengths for anime work: a broad toolset, including cartoon-oriented generation, references and editing in one environment.

Limitations and gaps: no official maximum duration or video resolution is stated, and the page notes that displayed pricing is for reference only and that final pricing requires signing in. The breadth of features and models can make it harder to know which one to use for a given shot.

Fits: creators who want images, video and avatars under one roof.

“Not stated” means the vendor page we reviewed did not say, not that the feature does not exist. Treat the table as a checklist of questions to answer in your own tests.

It depends on the job and on what you compare them with. Against a full studio production they are limited. Against having no animation at all, they change what a small team or solo creator can attempt.

Treat the generator as a fast prototyping and shot-making assistant, not a replacement for writing, direction and design. Audience reaction also varies, and some viewers are openly critical of AI-made work, so quality and disclosure both matter.

Most current generators are diffusion models extended to video. As in image diffusion, the model starts from random noise and removes it step by step, guided by your inputs. The extension to video, described in research such as Video Diffusion Models, is that the model denoises a whole sequence of frames together, so that motion and appearance stay coherent over time. Many systems work in a compressed latent space rather than on raw pixels to keep generation affordable.

Create an original character sheet with front, side and three-quarter views, a fixed color palette and a clear silhouette. Keep it simple: fewer fine details means less flicker.

Break the sequence into short shots with one action and one camera move each. A four-shot, twenty-second sequence is more realistic than one long generation.

Start each shot from a clean keyframe of your character. Where a tool supports first and last frames or reference images, use them to define the pose and keep identity stable.

Describe subject, action, camera and mood, and add a style anchor. For example: “The girl slowly turns toward the camera, wind moves her hair, petals drift past, gentle push-in, 2D cel shading, clean linework.”

Scrub through each clip for warped hands, drifting faces and flickering lines. Discard or regenerate failures instead of hoping nobody notices.

Join the best takes, color-match them, add sound and captions, and add disclosure where a platform requires it.

Developers can make the pipeline repeatable. Two patterns help: scripted post-processing with ffmpeg, and a resilient job-based client for any generation API.

The script below extracts a last frame to seed the next shot, normalizes clips to a common size and frame rate so they can be joined, concatenates them, and optionally holds each frame twice for an “on twos” look. Adjust sizes and frame rates to your project.

Video generation is usually asynchronous: you submit a job, then poll or receive a callback. The sketch below shows the pattern with a timeout and backoff. The endpoints and fields are placeholders, so follow your provider’s documentation for real ones.

Most are diffusion models for video. They start from noise and remove it step by step across a sequence of frames, guided by a text prompt and often a starting image or reference images, until coherent motion appears.

Usually, for consistency. The image locks the character and style, while the prompt only needs to describe motion.

Thin lines and flat color regions make small frame-to-frame changes visible. Simpler designs, slower motion and post-processing reduce it.

Partly. Reference images, saved characters and shared keyframes help, but you should still review every shot for drift.

For short clips, concept previews and storyboards, often yes. For long, dialogue-driven stories, not yet.

It depends on the tool and plan. Read the current terms and make sure you have rights to any characters or assets you upload.

Run the same character image and prompt through each, then compare consistency, line stability, motion quality and cost per usable second.

AI anime video generators are useful, but uneven. The five tools reviewed here differ in input modes, consistency tools, developer access and how much their own pages say, and none removes the core problems of consistency, flicker and limited acting. The practical approach is to test several tools on the same shots, keep your workflow tool-agnostic, design characters that are simple and consistent, and review every clip before it ships. Used that way, they are a capable prototyping and shot-making aid, not a substitute for design and direction.

#10706. Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 vs Watch 9: When is it right to spend more?

The Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is worth spending more on if you want longer battery life, a brighter outdoor display, and specialized features for trail running and diving. The Galaxy Watch 9 is the better fit if you want Samsung’s latest everyday health tools in a smaller, lighter watch.

Both, however, are fantastic smartwatches with limitless potential. They share a Snapdragon Wear Elite processor and many of Samsung’s newest wellness features, so deciding between them is really about figuring out your priorities when it comes to buying a wearable this holiday season. We’re here to help you figure that out.

As we covered in our Galaxy Watch 9 and Watch Ultra 2 comparison, both watches move to Qualcomm’s Snapdragon Wear Elite platform. Both also ship with Wear OS 7 and One UI 8 Watch.

That shared foundation includes useful health insights. Vitals brings overnight measurements together to establish your personal baseline, while Daily Cardio Load helps you understand training demands and recovery. Hearing monitoring can flag potentially unsafe noise levels, whether you’re at a concert or somewhere unexpectedly loud.

Both also get five years of Wear OS updates and security patches, making either a useful long-term partner for your Samsung device, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra or Galaxy Z Fold8.

The clearest differences are size, battery capacity, display brightness, and outdoor capabilities. Here’s how the lineup breaks down:

As you can tell, the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is considerably bigger and heavier, but you get a lot more capability for that additional size.

The Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 makes its strongest case when your plans regularly take you beyond a desk, a gym, or a charger.

Samsung rates the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for up to 60 hours with the always-on display enabled, compared with up to 30 hours for the Watch 9, and that extra headroom is appealing when your day starts with a workout and finishes well into the evening.

Then there’s the screen. In our hands-on with both watches, the Ultra 2’s brightness stood out immediately, with a higher peak that gives it an advantage when checking workout information outdoors, even in the bright sun. And its titanium frame provides added durability.

The Ultra 2 also adds Trail Run tracking for elevation and terrain impact, route guidance using compatible GPX files, and Nutrition Alert hydration guidance. These are tangible reasons to upgrade if your usual run involves trails instead of pavement.

Diving is another differentiator. Samsung specifies recreational diving support to depths of up to 40 meters, with automatic tracking of depth, dive time and water temperature — a remarkable feat for a piece of technology that lives on your wrist.

The best choice becomes clearer when you picture your own routine:

Choose the Galaxy Watch 9 for everyday wellness, a lighter fit, and strong value. Choose the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for longer adventures, more demanding outdoor activities, and greater freedom between charges.

If you’re replacing an older Galaxy Watch, both offer a compelling new generation of health insights and long-term support. Both last a long time and look absolutely awesome with running shorts or a business suit. But the Watch Ultra 2 can take you that extra mile, literally and figuratively, and may be worth spending more on during Best Buy’s Holiday Deals event.

#10704. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Colin Klein and Andrew Barron. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10703. Rockstar explains why GTA 6 added its Criminal Profile system

Rockstar Games’ head of development has explained why GTA 6 tracks how players commit crimes, saying the idea grew out of lessons the studio learned while making Red Dead Redemption 2.

Unlike previous GTA games, GTA 6’s protagonists each have their own Criminal Profile that changes based on how they behave across Leonida.

Pulling off robberies without unnecessary violence can move a character toward Professional, while attacking civilians and going on prolonged rampages can push them toward Irredeemable.

These ratings then impact how aggressively police respond and how each character replenishes their Focus meter, the latter being important for improving your aim in gunfights.

While the consensus on the new system has been mixed amongst the community, Rockstar Games’ head of development Rob Nelson has now revealed the reasons behind its inclusion.

Speaking to Eurogamer, Nelson said Rockstar wanted to bring what players do in the open world closer to how Jason and Lucia are portrayed in the story.

That meant giving players more choices in everyday encounters, right down to normal GTA actions like robbing an NPC. Nelson said the team was aiming to “build up the sophistication of the NPC population, and also allow for more player choice” while also adding “more options in terms of the way you interact with the world beyond just violence.”

After all, the results of going on a rampage in GTA 6 can follow you long after you lose the cops. In fact, repeated acts of extreme violence can permanently lock your Criminal Profile at Irredeemable. However, Nelson mentions that it also allows for further freedom in your actions.

“It’s still a world of violence, but you could threaten somebody by aiming a gun at them and just robbing them, and they have a chance to comply. So you don’t have to beat them to death to get their money,” Nelson explained.

Rockstar also wanted to create a more realistic approach to player actions, building upon Red Dead Redemption 2’s Honor system.

“What if, instead of whipping an RPG out of nowhere, you have to call your car. You have to pop a car trunk and get it, and now I’m walking, and now people are noticing, but they’re sort of sidling away, and it’s more believable,” the developer continued.

“[Now] there’s people who also have guns, and they’re shooting at you, or you try to hold somebody up, and they pull a gun, or they grab it, and then you have to deal with them.”

It’s certainly a bold change for a series built around causing chaos, but it’s part of the reason why GTA 6 feels so much more alive than previous titles. So much so that Cyberpunk 2’s creative director even joked that the sequel is “cancelled” after seeing what Rockstar has achieved.

“He’s getting old, so weight loss isn’t advisable. The priority is to maintain his current condition and keep him healthy,” they said.
