#10629. What if Vampire Survivors met Grand Theft Auto 2? Maniac is a brilliant mash-up that dares to ask that question

"We all have a pathetic hot-dog-suit guy inside of us."

What would you get if you combined the chaotic top-down action of old-school Grand Theft Auto 2 with the chaotic top-down action of Vampire Survivors? That's the question Soldat and King Arthur's Gold developer Transhuman asked when it started working on 2024's roguelite shooter Maniac. The result is a delight, yet Maniac remains frustratingly underappreciated.

Your goal is simple: survive for as long as you can or until the 20-minute clock hits zero. Throughout that time, your secondary objective is to create as much mayhem as possible - painting the road with the blood of your foes, slamming cars into buildings, performing drive-bys as you rampage across the procedurally-generated city. The more carnage you cause, the higher your Wanted level, until you're fending off police and the military.

Maniac's debt to GTA is clear, and its chaotic action is brilliantly realised as it channels the compelling rhythms of a Survivors-style roguelite, where randomness in items and level layout makes each run fresh as you strive to unlock new characters, progressing a little further each time. It's a relatively slight experience, as its £4 price tag reflects, featuring a single compact map, just six characters, and barely any permanent progression. But Maniac makes a spirited argument for a return to a simpler time when GTA was all about shooting cops, stealing cars, and indiscriminately murdering pedestrians rather than the 'everything' game it is today.

Maniac is also a game that feels like it's trying to make good on an idea many of us had when we first played GTA 1 & 2 all those years ago: what would a better-looking, more advanced version of that look like? And for developer Michał Marcinkowski, Maniac's genesis also harkens back to an earlier time. "When I was 12, I used to come back from school and play the demo of GTA 1," he tells me over email. "It was top-down and time-limited, and all I'd do was try to cause as much chaos as possible within the 20 minutes of the demo."

And there's perhaps a hint of a 12-year-old's exuberance to Maniac. There's the bright colour palette and gleeful chaos of its physics-driven mechanics, the adrenaline rush its aggressive enemy AI creates, and its absurd roster of playable characters, which range from Little Girl to Hotdog Guy. "Each [character] is a bit of me and a bit of you," Marcinkowski explains. "We all have a drunk Santa in us. We all have a pathetic hot-dog-suit guy inside of us. Each character failed at something and is lashing out."

As each new Grand Theft Auto game pushes further into realism, the idea of recreating the rampages seen in classic GTA feels increasingly unpalatable, but Maniac's colourful cartoon aesthetic means it can get away with more. One Achievement, with a flourish of old-school bad-taste humour, challenges players to destroy five school buses using an airstrike ability called Santa's Little Helpers. And that level of irreverent, chaotic lunacy escalates the deeper into each session you get, particularly as your Wanted level climbs: if you antagonise law enforcement enough here, they eventually break out a game-ending nuke.

"This comes back to being 12," Marcinkowski says. "Nukes are just cool. I wanted the game to end with a bang, and what's more maniacal and exaggerated than a nuke? So that idea was with me for more than 20 years."

Core to Maniac's design is the ability to change from a Survivors-like auto-shooter to a standard shooter where you're able to control your aim. The latter option is, I think, the more fitting one, particularly given how much driving you do. If you squint, it almost feels like a GTA 2 clone. And while the Vampire Survivors inspirations are plentiful - including its now-standard three-option upgrade system - the GTA elements are clearly the more prevalent.

It might not play exactly like a GTA game, but it certainly captures the series' early days spirit. And Marcinkowski says he's surprised more developers haven't tried to riff on GTA: "You'd expect the most popular game in the world to have hundreds of clones, but that's not the case." There may be a reason for that.

As big as modern-day GTA is, clones are apparently much less popular. "When you look at the sales data, particularly of top-down GTA clones, they don't sell well", explains Marcinkowski. "I found that out way too late, and I thought Maniac wouldn't be a hit." His publisher was initially equally buoyant too. "But then they studied the market and stopped being so hyped. That's how it felt as we got close to release. The wishlist count wasn't very high."

"I was expecting 10,000 [copies sold]," Marcinkowski tells me. "I actually started making another game two months before Maniac came out because I thought I'd go broke." But then the game launched, and early worries proved unfounded. It sold more than 100K copies, comfortably blowing past its maker's modest expectations, putting it in the top one percent of games released on Steam.

It's a surprise success story built on the foundations of Marcinkowski's youth, and as we chat about GTA, he reveals he's less keen on Rockstar's modern games, which lack that classic gameplay drive. "I always liked the counter-culture side of GTA, which is why I put so much of it into Maniac," he elaborates. "GTA 6 seems to have become pop culture, so it's lost its appeal for me." Marcinkowski's heart, it seems, is old-school.

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

#10628. OnePlus starts shutting down its website to ‘focus on customer support’

After shutting down its US operations earlier this year, OnePlus has quietly closed its website in some regions as it shifts to “focus on customer support.”

OnePlus announced in July that it would end new product launches in the US and Europe. Just weeks later, the OnePlus 15 and other products the brand was selling in the US were sold out for good and, more recently, OnePlus rolled out its Android 17 update to transition devices to Oppo’s ColorOS instead of OxygenOS.

Now, OnePlus has fully pulled the plug on its US website, as well as some other regions.

oneplus.com/us has purged all product listings, including for accessories, with a new message saying that the brand is “streamlining” the site:

Following our July 16, 2026 announcement, OnePlus is streamlining this website to focus on customer support for existing OnePlus devices. For warranty, repair, and other support, please visit our support site Click.

The same applies to the OnePlus website in the Canada, and other regions, though the UK and EU countries such as France still have an operational site that mostly push Oppo products over remaining OnePlus devices, a practice that started a few months ago prior to the shutdown. The OnePlus 15, for example, is no longer available to purchase in the UK at all. Presumably, though, the OnePlus website will shut down in these regions eventually as well.

The OnePlus website in India is unaffected and still selling devices.

The warranty website OnePlus links to appears to be more than a bit out of date, with a focus on 2023’s OnePlus Open. The most recent device mentioned is the OnePlus 13, though the page has been updated based on what we’re able to see on the Wayback Machine.

#10626. I've never known a moment like this in video games

As gaming captivates the world, it seems fitting that Grand Theft Auto - its most contradictory, chaotic, and all-encompassing product - is to act as its ambassador.

It was hard not to spend the first hour or so of Grand Theft Auto 6's launch day in a kind of stun-lock. As far as video games editorial goes - which is not that far, in fairness; prepare for an over-flattering analogy - the effect was roughly the equivalent of Tom Hanks' experience, as his John H. Miller first set foot on Omaha beach. Tweets and reels whistled overhead, news leads stung the ears, exploding search terms blew apart some poor guides team ally on one side, while new discoveries in the early game - made by the millions and millions of launch-day players all picking it apart at once - appeared by surprise like some flanking ambush on the other.

The analogy falls apart pretty quickly when you think about it: the heroism of writing a little article about a video game aside, the frenzy of this week has been a joy, not a horror. But the point is, I've been doing this job for over a decade now, paying attention to video games for another two beyond that, and I've truly never seen anything like this before. It has been totally overwhelming. And then I thought about that for a moment before realising that - duh - nobody has ever seen anything like this before. GTA 6 is the biggest entertainment release of all time, and it's not even remotely close.

It's worth taking a moment just to actually, properly take stock of all the headlines from the past couple of days in terms of the sheer breadth and scale of its launch. Parent company Take Two announced yesterday that GTA 6 sold 32.5m copies before it came out. By the end of its first day on sale, it had made a record-obliterating $3.79bn. By contrast, the once record-breaking GTA 5 made a once astounding $800m in the same period; the "opening" period of the largest film of all time, Avengers: Endgame, made $1.22bn, and that's totalling box office receipts across its release day until the end of the first Sunday after it.

It has set multiple records for streaming, per StreamCharts, with 214.63m hours watched in its first two days - significantly more than the previous biggest game, Valorant, achieved with 150m hours in its entire launch week. It's garnered more watch time in those two days than Elden Ring, Hogwarts Legacy, and Cyberpunk 2077 achieved with their entire launch weeks combined. Its concurrent viewers peak so far - 6.38 million people - is nearly four times the previous record of 1.74m, again formerly held by Valorant.

It crashed Reddit. More than 2 million UK and US Xbox players used the 'New Zealand trick' to play the game early, which is more than a third of that country's entire population. Internet exchange operator DE-CIX claimed its launch led to the highest amount of concurrent internet usage in history, at 33.03 terabits per second.

On top of all those raw numbers, it's also just felt utterly inescapable. I can't open Instagram, TikTok, BlueSky or X without being blasted with memes, viral clips of early discoveries, reactions, hot takes, feeble attempts at brand piggybacks, slightly wayward early-game tips from opportunist streamers who haven't escaped the first chapter, or makeup tutorials for looking like Lucia. I can't open my inbox without scrolling through reams of tenuous PR pitches for stories with barely the faintest connection to the game. I can't open my phone without messages from parents, friends or peers. Even the mainstream news - heaven forbid! - has gradually clocked onto this whole video games thing, this unknown artifact from the imaginary world of "online", maybe being a bit important. Dozens of my colleagues have been inundated with last-minute call-ups to provide an expert soundbite (they're so desperate they even got me on at one point).

We'll have a lot more to say about this game in the coming days and weeks (with apologies to regulars who are already sick of it - counterprogramming starts this Monday!). It's the richest of texts, a near endless comedy clip generator, a total technical marvel and all the rest - you already know. But it felt worth it to just sit still for a moment, and take stock of what's happening here.

This is the single biggest moment any piece of entertainment media has ever had. But more than that it's felt like a kind of party. The vibes in some corners are orgiastic, as streamers uncover spooky twists, outlandish reactivity, and easter eggs I'd never got close to in a hundred-odd hours with the thing - all of this pure dynamite for the audience. Given the budgets involved and the particularly unique blend of circumstances around it - the changing shape of video game funding; of how attention is monopolised; of how development may happen in the future and the sheer amount of time and subsequent anticipation built up since the previous entry - we will likely be waiting decades for another moment like it. If there's ever a moment like it again. Part of the sense of ecstasy going around comes, perhaps subconsciously, from the acknowledgment that in pure hype and attention terms, this might be as good as it ever gets.

For the briefest time, the world belongs to video games. Rockstar and Take-Two's executives and shareholders will no doubt reap the greatest rewards from all this - all part of the great irony that makes this game's central premise all the more interesting. But I sincerely hope its developers - especially the 34 fired by the company last year, now awaiting the outcome of their tribunal over alleged union-busting, and those who hope to form a union from the currently open vote - are able to feel a particular sense of pride.

Just as this launch has been unprecedented in its success, the last two or three years have been unprecedented in their grimness for video game workers across most of the world, certainly the once-dominat western part. If we're very lucky - and frankly, I have my doubts - this burst of attention and visible profitability might go some way to helping the industry claw its way back, perhaps via attracting, as a knock-on from all the new-found attention, some kind of more sustainable investment. If we're not, and this is really the last behemoth, the last video game monument of its kind, the last rave before the police come raiding at dawn, at least we've gone out with one hell of a bang.

GTA 6 - again, it's been said before now - is such a curious thing. A game of goofiness, self-awareness, prestige, pretentiousness, childish humour, astute commentary, ironic cultural criticism, surprising player expression and - above all of that - the most mind-boggling, brain-scrambling level of profoundly human craft. There's something kind of perfect about this.

It's a good thing, actually, that video games' greatest export is also its most contradictory. Video games are contradictory things in themselves. If the non-specialist media might still occasionally slip into treating video games like some kind of object from outer space, then we might as well put forward the best possible candidate for a capsule. It's the same way you'd think about it if you had to stick one thing in a vacuum-sealed box, to send out into the universe in an attempt to symbolise humanity: something that summarises close to all of it; that we're proud of; and crucially, that captures the spirit of it too.

There are many more that might've put a more respectable foot forward - certainly that have a less chequered history, or maybe a more sophisticated, nuanced, or inventive use of the form. But there's really no better video game for exemplifying the full, chaotic range of the medium. It seems right, when you think about it, for GTA to be the one out there sitting under the spotlight, in the biggest moment this silly, serious artform might ever have.

#10623. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10622. New programmable photonic chip can control how fast light moves

Researchers at Seoul National University and the University of Seoul have developed a programmable photonic integrated circuit that can slow light whenever needed.

The team was led by Professors Namkyoo Park and Sunkyu Yu of the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at Seoul National University, working with Professor Xianji Piao of the School of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the University of Seoul.

Slowing Light Could Help Solve a Computing Bottleneck

The rapid growth of generative AI and large scale AI models has sharply increased the amount of computing power required by data centers and servers. Conventional electronic semiconductors are struggling to keep pace because they consume large amounts of energy and face limits in how quickly they can transmit data.

These challenges have intensified interest in optical computing, which uses light rather than electrical signals to process information. Optical systems could potentially move data at extremely high speeds while using less power.

However, light also presents a major challenge. Because it naturally moves at a fixed speed, it is difficult to delay optical signals or temporarily hold them in place. Those capabilities are essential for creating buffers and memory functions in optical computers.

To address this problem, the researchers designed a programmable photonic circuit that can control both the speed and shape of optical signals. Their approach provides more flexibility over "slow light" than previously proposed methods.

The study was published in the renowned international journal Advanced Science.

Why Optical Signals Sometimes Need To Wait

Photonic integrated circuits are emerging as a promising technology for processing information quickly and efficiently with light. In data centers, optical communication networks, and future computing systems, moving signals rapidly is only part of the challenge.

Systems must also ensure that different signals arrive at the correct time. In some cases, a light signal must be delayed so it can remain synchronized with other information moving through the system.

One method for creating these delays relies on coupled-resonator-induced transparency (CRIT), which uses interference among several optical resonators.

CRIT allows light within a selected frequency range to pass through a device while also reducing the speed at which the optical signal travels.

Traditional CRIT devices usually have operating characteristics that become permanent once they are manufactured. This makes it difficult to change how they function after fabrication.

For example, engineers who want to create a longer signal delay or work with a different frequency range often need to design and manufacture an entirely new photonic device.

That lack of adaptability increases the complexity of optical communication hardware and data center infrastructure. It can also raise costs and extend development schedules whenever new capabilities are needed.

The problem is especially important for AI servers and next-generation data centers, where enormous amounts of information must be processed in real time. Fixed optical components have therefore remained a major obstacle to more practical optical computing systems.

A Programmable Design for Controlling Light

The research team developed a different strategy by treating two optical states in CRIT systems, known as the bright mode and dark mode, as one unified degree of freedom. The researchers also added two controllable loop couplers.

Together, these changes created a new design principle for programmable photonic integrated circuits. Resonator arrangements that were previously locked into one configuration after fabrication could instead be adjusted for different purposes.

Using the new CRIT structure, the researchers showed that the movement of light could be delayed and controlled as needed. They also demonstrated that interference between the bright and dark modes could be handled as a single integrated design parameter.

This approach greatly expanded the flexibility of photonic resonator circuits that had previously been limited by fixed designs.

Controlling Delay, Bandwidth, and Signal Shape

The researchers theoretically demonstrated that the two loop couplers could be used to adjust the bandwidth and shape of the passband. They could also control how long signals were delayed and how efficiently those signals traveled through the circuit.

This means that both the speed and transmission behavior of optical signals could be reconfigured across entire systems containing multiple resonators, rather than only within a single resonator.

Numerical simulations also showed that the speed of optical pulses could be adjusted dynamically while the circuit was operating.

The results indicated that signal delay times could be changed without reducing processing performance. The system could also convert the frequency of light without requiring additional specialized components.

Simulations Suggest the Chip Could Be Practical

The researchers used three-dimensional electromagnetic simulations to test whether the CRIT device could be built on a silicon nitride (Si3N4) photonic integrated circuit platform.

They also evaluated a range of real-world issues that could affect the device during manufacturing and operation. These included material losses, differences in resonator quality, backscattering, coupling fluctuations, phase errors in the loop couplers, and thermal crosstalk.

The simulations indicated that the proposed structure could continue to operate reliably under realistic conditions.

One Chip Could Perform Several Optical Functions

The study introduces a programmable photonic platform that can control both the timing and frequency properties of light signals in real time.

The design could overcome the limitations of conventional optical delay devices, which typically perform only fixed functions. It also suggests that several important capabilities could eventually be combined within one photonic circuit.

These functions include signal synchronization, adjustable delay lines, optical buffers, and frequency conversion.

The same design principles may also be useful beyond CRIT systems. The researchers believe the approach could be applied to a broad range of photonic circuits based on resonators, potentially providing a foundation for more adaptable optical signal processing technologies.

Potential Benefits for AI and Data Centers

If the technology is commercialized, a single programmable optical chip could perform several tasks, including controlling signal speed and switching between different functions.

In that sense, the chip could operate in a way similar to a software-defined system, with its behavior adjusted according to changing needs.

This flexibility could help data centers and AI servers process information more efficiently while reducing energy consumption.

Combining several signal processing functions on one chip could also make optical communication equipment and sensor systems smaller and less expensive.

Over the longer term, the technology could support industries that depend on extremely fast information processing, including autonomous driving, next-generation communications, and quantum technologies.

Researchers Plan Larger Programmable Photonic Systems

Professor Namkyoo Park, co-corresponding author of the study from Seoul National University, stated, "This research is significant in that it proposes a new design principle that allows the flow of light within photonic integrated circuits to be reconfigured as needed, greatly enhancing design flexibility. We plan to expand this technology toward large-scale programmable photonic integrated circuits based on silicon photonics and photonic AI technologies."

Co-first authors Dr. Seungkyun Park and Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae, who led the theoretical framework and numerical analysis, added, "Through this study, we realized that reinterpreting conventional photonic resonator physics from a different perspective can serve as a starting point for discovering new functionalities in photonic integrated circuits. We plan to further develop this research toward practical device implementation and experimental validation."

Dr. Seungkyun Park is affiliated with the InnoCORE PICORE Center at KAIST and is currently conducting research on photonic AI and quantum optics at the Photonic Systems Laboratory, Seoul National University.

Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae is conducting research on programmable photonic integrated circuits at the Intelligent Wave Systems Laboratory, SNU.

The research received support from the Ministry of Science and ICT through the Innovative Research Center (IRC) program, the Basic Research Laboratory (BRL) program, and the Young Researcher Program.

Dr. Seungkyun Park also participated in the study with support from the InnoCORE program (PICORE Center).

Materials provided by Seoul National University College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10621. "It stayed alive for so long" - Behind the wheel with the Last Riders saying goodbye to GTA Online, amidst GTA 6's world-changing launch

In the decade or so since Grand Theft Auto Online's arrival, its roads have become a kind of home; well-loved by its racers, its gear heads, and speed freaks. It's a game that allows players to lose themselves in the roleplay, the heists, and the crime sprees. But for me, an occasional dipper into its major updates, the car folk have always been the engine driving its appeal.

So when the GTA Online community decided to host Last Ride events this week - a sort of mass in-game road trip and a communal farewell before attention inevitably turned to GTA 6 - I was keen to join in. The Last Drive was scheduled to begin an hour before GTA 6's launch, and would see numerous groups embarking on their own journey across GTA Online's servers, all taking the same route: starting at Los Santos' Del Perro Pier and following the highway around Los Santos' entire perimeter before finally rolling up to its airport. Players would then fly off into the sunset, close the game, and move on. Some just for a while, others perhaps forever.

Kirsty is one of the many long-time players who chose to embark on their own last drive as part of the event. She runs the Fast Crew racing community in GTA Online and has been playing since the beginning in 2013, even meeting her fiance through the game. Unlike some, however, she plans to stick around in GTA Online after GTA 6's release, at least until the new game's equivalent comes along.

It's the social dynamic of GTA Online that makes it special, Kirsty tells me when we speak online, and she calls it a community of players "that thrives from supporting each other, racing together and just having fun". She points to GTA Online's 30-player sessions as one reason for its enduring appeal "That's important for people in communities like ours," she explains. "Gaming is an escape to many people, a distraction or a coping method. GTA is special because it stayed alive for so long, giving people that escape."

Then there's Brittany, who leads the GTA Online Ascent racing community. Brittany has a real-life kart racing background, and built race tracks across various Xbox 360 games before arriving in GTA Online. As with many of the players I spoke with, the in-game connections Brittany made have blossomed into real-life friendships, all built around racing.

"GTA Online introduced me to many lifelong friends," they explain. "Hell, I even met my girlfriend Ari through the GTA Racing community. Every year, five-to-six of us go to the Indy 500 and all of us met through my [GTA Online] PC racing community BRITCAR." Brittany was also planning their own Last Drive, using old cars from the game's earlier years.

It's clear GTA Online has become more than just a game to many people, and I was eager to see how players might choose to say goodbye. So as GTA 6 loomed, I booted up GTA Online and prepared for my own Last Drive, waiting near the pier in a random online lobby. Slowly, other people started to arrive - all seemingly strangers - and at the allotted time, we slowly began making our way around the map as one.

What I saw was brilliant. A snaking line of customised cars, custom paint jobs, and underlighting, slowly navigating the island; a peaceful, reverential convoy of players who weren't blasting away at each other with weapons for once. The chaos of the typical GTA Online lobby never broke out. Instead, there was a sort of unspoken armistice between those eager to enjoy one last moment in a game they'd spent so much time in. Gradually, more people joined our conga line.

Besides being a lovely way to close a chapter for many, it allowed for a cheeky bit of peacocking too. Many players pulled up in truly exceptional rides: there were supercars, bulky muscle cars, and street-racer lightshows, all as much an expression of their owners' personalities and tastes as any human avatar.

At one point, the leader of this snaking pack made a wrong turn, taking us into a dockyard and an unfortunate dead-end. We then all had to slowly reverse out - a time-consuming, if amusing, process which allowed one opportunistic spectator to pull up and take a few of us out with a well-placed RPG. But after we'd all reconvened nearby, the respectful atmosphere resumed. No flying Oppressor Mk II rained missiles on us, and there weren't any kamikaze crashes interrupting our final drive.

When we did finally reach the airport, people left their vehicles and things took a more knockabout turn. Together we huddled for a few moments, letting off gunfire and fireworks into the night. Then the cops came, and the group rushed to steal planes and helicopters before things got wilder still.

Slowly, eventually our group began to disperse. Players either went on to cause some final havoc before GTA 6's arrival, or they did as I did, flying over the ocean into the sunset before leaving the server once and for all. It sounds sappy but it was moving. Here, a game perhaps best known for its player-versus-player hijinks had - thanks to a close-knit community consisting of people like Kirsty, Brittany, and all their friends - became a wholesome drive along. A send off to the game as it was, before ushering in a new dawn.

It speaks to what is special about GTA Online, really. A playground people have spent years in, clearly still important to them, and a chance to say a farewell of sorts from the place they feel most comfortable: behind the wheel.

#10619. I love YouTube on TV, but we desperately need better watchlist controls

Managing a YouTube watchlist shouldn’t be as frustrating as it is, especially on the most-used app in my life. Google’s premier streaming app has too many issues with resurfacing content; in my opinion, this is now a major problem that needs to be fixed.

Aside from my YouTube watchlist gripes, it’s no secret that moving from a web browser to the TV is ultimately still a frustrating experience.

Lots of things I love about watching videos on my laptop or PC just don’t translate well to when I’m sat on my sofa holding a remote trying to find something to watch after wrapping up for the day. One thing I’m getting annoyed with is how YouTube scales on TV and Android TV boxes because you’re given less choice and arguably poorer recommendations for content due to the constraints in place.

Understandably, you can’t compress the layout too much on a TV as you’re typically sat a few feet away and all aspects of the interface need to be legible. I would still like to tweak it, though, if that were possible.

Maybe we could get proper sections for content, like how Netflix and other streaming platforms offer categorized areas where you can search for “comedy” or “horror”. I’m almost certain that, with Google’s insane investment and massive AI push, where every video is already analyzed hundreds of times, that wouldn’t be too difficult to do.

The problem now is that the sidebar menu is just too broad. Gaming covers basically everything. Then each line of videos accounts for one title, but requires search and previous watch data to populate. I find myself often fighting with recommendations on my TV, whereas on desktop it’s not as much of a problem.

That leads me to my biggest problem with YouTube, and it’s the, frankly, embarrassing watchlist or “Watch later” controls.

I can’t fault that it works, but right now the watchlist function is like something out of the early 2000s due to the lack of flexibility that I touched on above. As my YouTube consumption has increased 20x over the past four or five years, managing what I want to come back to or might have missed the first time around is starting to become a bit of a chore.

I’ve tried using playlists to save things, but things get messy very quickly because of how cumbersome it is to a) create a playlist on TV and b) how playlists are organized by default.

Google TV isn’t perfect, but it’s where I keep my biggest TV and movie watchlist outside of YouTube. Yes, YouTube is mostly for user-generated content, but I would take something as basic as Google TV’s interface baked into YouTube somehow. Add in categorization and auto-clear.

The Watch Later section has become an unwieldy place that I never really want to go unless I desperately have to. I’m constantly losing videos that I hoped to come back to or have an affinity for.

Videos don’t clear automatically, nor do you know when they were added. Yes, I’ll give credit that you can sort by a few options, but that’s the bare minimum. The process of moving things around is slow and takes multiple taps. Watch Later is also hidden compared to other services. It’s under your account, then beneath “Liked videos,” which is below any you may have uploaded yourself.

On a TV, the process is so annoying I just avoid it altogether. You go to the sidebar menu > Library > Watch later. It should be front-and-center on a TV. It’s almost unforgivable in my eyes because even your watch history is given greater prominence. I simply cannot understand that decision.

I’m not always a huge fan of AI being shoehorned into apps and services, but this is one of those areas that has been left to rot for a while and could get a big uplift from adding in a little bit of Gemini. Even if it were just an enhanced search function or more tools for organizing.

With YouTube TV effectively taking over regular broadcast television and being the world’s biggest streaming platform, I’m shocked by how so many areas of the platform remain in a consistently neglected state.

A little bit of spit and polish, and maybe I’ll start using this section again. For now, though, I’ll stick to texting myself links of stuff I want to watch. The ball is in your court, Google.

#10617. Queen's Domain review

Where to start with Queen's Domain? It's tempting to begin with King's Field; after all, FromSoftware's seminal first-person action-RPG is an explicit inspiration for developer Freshly Baked Games. But 40 hours later, it feels like so much more. It's equally tempting to invoke another FromSoftware game: Dark Souls. Queen's Domain owes an obvious debt to that game's celebrated level design: the dazzling interconnectedness of its vertiginous world. But that's not quite right either. The deeper you delve, the more there's something of the spirit of The Legend of Zelda, a touch of Metroidvania too. Until finally you concede that Queen's Domain, with its remarkable world of densely packed secrets, is very much its own.

All this begins with an island. You, Prince Beruim, are a castaway, washed ashore on Neasied. Your father, the king, has disappeared, and your search has brought you, somewhat circuitously, here. As you clamber to your feet and survey the island, it makes an immediately striking impression: all strange peaks and crooked spires; a cramped, chaotic mass of landmarks lost amid the dense undergrowth. Pyramids perch atop steep plateaus; a vast, moss-tinged mausoleum sprawls across the land to one side while the other is dominated by an imposing tower. It's a world made all the stranger by Queen's Domain's retro aesthetic. Here, shimmering low-res textures and low-poly shapes converge to create elaborate spaces and structures that feel slightly at odds with the lo-fi vibe. It's not quite old, not quite new; instead, something of a world lost outside of time. It makes for an enigmatic, disorientating landscape. And when paired with a soundtrack that delights in discordance, as it swings from gentle ambience to pulsing synths, it's a mood that refuses to resolve.

There is so much to say about Neasied. And so much I absolutely will not spoil. But very early impressions don't do it justice at all. As you take your first tentative steps onto the island, presented with a forking path that eventually winds back in on itself, it's easy to mistake Queen's Domain for a first-person Dark Souls - especially if, like me, you're only passingly familiar with King's Field. As in most Soulslikes, early progress is defined by a punishing inch-by-inch crawl from one brutal encounter to the next; where death - via chicken-cricket hybrids, ladybird-rats, and other wonderfully odd things - comes fast and frequently, respawning you back at the last Crystal Monument you visited. And because its wilfully old-school design eschews modern-day niceties such as a combat lock-on or dedicated roll button, there's a looseness to fighting that takes a while to click. But it does, slowly, click.

This is a game that demands you lean into its limitations, encourages you to find ways to make them work. At first, those Souls-honed instincts are likely to be your undoing as you naturally scramble for a dodge button that isn't there. But soon a different sort of pattern emerges: you realise that to avoid a ladybird attack, you simply need to step back - but not so far you don't have time to nip in and strike while your opponent takes a pause. A charging enemy might require a different approach - a well-timed backstep and swivelling sideways strafe to put you in the right place for a counter. And in this way, combat becomes a compelling sort of puzzle as you learn each of the 100+ enemies' little ticks - including rock, paper, scissors-style weaknesses to different weapon types - and figure out how to respond.

There's more to consider. Your melee damage output, for instance, is directly tied to your stamina bar: the more stamina you have, the harder you'll hit; too low and it's a waste of a swing. That forces you to choose your moments carefully, moving out to safety while your bar refills. And there's a charge attack, also linked to your stamina, that does more damage still. Already combat has a distinctively deliberate rhythm, but then come your first throwing knives. These are most immediately useful against enemies ordinarily out of reach - like the laser-spewing foliage dogging your journey early on - but they're equally valuable for luring individual enemies away from the throng. Throwing weapons take a while to return to your pouch, meaning they too need to be deployed with purpose. And while knives are just the start - you'll acquire an increasingly exotic selection of throwables including poison-tipped darts and homing blades - they all fundamentally work the same way.

And the interplay of these various warm-ups and cooldowns creates an increasingly engaging rhythm as you learn to juggle between your variously equipped weapons to maximise their damage potential. You might, for instance, fire off a one-two-three volley of homing darts, then use a magic lantern to toss out a massive fireball that bounces destructively around the room. And it's there, in the ensuing chaos, you'll charge up your blade, letting rip then returning to your darts again. Combat might encourage a methodical pace, but that doesn't mean you can't fight fast, and there are some fascinating synergies among the various weapons, many having their own wonderfully distinct mechanisms. There's a brawling gauntlet that lifts you clean off the ground and a vampiric blade capable of stealing an enemy's life force. There are hammers, creatures, and an unexpected mid-game weapon class, utilising a third rechargeable power source, that I am definitely not spoiling. Queen's Domain's 100 or so weapons create ample space for experimentation and customisation, and the further from its cautious beginnings you stray, the brisker, the more exhilarating its combat becomes. In my current, late-game form, I'm a whirl of blades and blasts and up-close attacks that feels anything but Soulslike.

Queen's Domain isn't just a nippier sort of game, though. It quickly reveals itself to be friendlier, more accommodating. Levelling, for instance, happens passively as you play, meaning every swing feels like it's contributing to your progress. Weapons level independently too, encouraging specialisation. There are no flasks, no accumulated currency you'll need to worry about dropping on death, no subsequent corpse runs. And progress can be saved at any time using the nearest Crystal Monument, meaning that when you respawn, you won't have to grind through the same opponents again. Enemies do eventually reappear of their own accord, so you can't exploit the save system indefinitely. But unlike Souls-style bonfires, Crystal Monuments only repopulate the world when you explicitly tell them to - when you Rest to clear status effects and restore health - giving you considerably more agency. You can decide when to push forward, when to retreat and lock in your progress, and when to reap the benefits of a rest and reset. Together, it makes for a breezier, less punishing, more consistently rewarding riff on the traditional Souslike, while never losing that I-can-do-this just-one-more-turn allure.

But as immensely enjoyable as Queen's Domain's combat is, the real draw here, unquestionably, is the world. Neasied is extraordinary. Right from the start, as you pick yourself off the sand and survey the landscape, its enigmatic huddle of peculiar landmarks isn't just intriguing, it's inviting. These odd pyramids, these curious plateaus practically demand to be explored. But how? The answer comes perhaps an hour in when, fancily clad skeleton boss successfully slain, you get your hands on the Holy Sword Endu. It's an immediate boon, far more lethal than the rubbish rusty blade you've been wielding so far. And its secondary attack - a long-range jab that propels you forward - is handy too. It's also your key to unlocking Neasied.

Endu's violent forward momentum can, you see, fling you across previously uncrossable gaps, gradually carrying you further and further as more upgrades reveal themselves. Suddenly, Queen's Domain takes flight, quite literally, becoming something closer to an exploratory sandbox as it adopts the spirited acrobatics of a platform game. And as you experiment with your newfound freedom, the more paths you explore, the island begins to take on a dizzying new form. Neasied, when viewed from up high, reveals itself to be a vastly intricate traversal puzzle. That peculiar structure in the distance? If you can find an appropriate ledge to fling yourself from, it's now yours for the investigating - and from there, one, two, three more tantalising branches are revealed. Neasied is an astonishing piece of design: a ludicrous, elaborate tangle of hidden pathways and secrets, forever folding in and around itself, that's crafted to invite curiosity and then constantly reward.

Freshly Baked Games always feels one step ahead. I can't recall a single moment where my exploratory What-ifs - What if I throw myself this way? What if I do a falling mid-air turn? What if I toss myself down this hole? - didn't bear fruit. Sometimes your prize is a steaming platter of boon-granting food, sometimes it's another bit of increasingly exotic weaponry. Perhaps you'll find a strange NPC with a favour to ask; fish eggs and mysterious skulls; a shopkeeper with a thing for ducks (who, in a typically lovely touch, festoons his store with the goods you're willing to sell), and so many mysterious new puzzle threads to pull. As you fling and probe and push on forward, that thing you've been carrying around for hours - or, conversely, that thing you saw hours ago - suddenly finds new purpose. There's a definite touch of Metroidvania as old areas reveal new truths the further you go. And because there's so little handholding, so little explicit direction, every discovery feels like your own.

But just as you think you're starting to get a handle on Queen's Domain's shape, along comes another surprise: dungeons as vastly intricate as the world outside. You'll navigate labyrinthine waterlogged caverns of angry fish where progress involves puzzling past ancient mechanisms. Later, an arcane tower where gargoyles prowl. Here, the convoluted upward route takes circuitous laps among the endless bookshelves, spilling out the windows onto teetering precipices with alarming sea views. Then down, however briefly, to eerie chambers below. Suddenly, the mood is less Soulslike, more Zelda, and your expectations shift again. And still new discoveries lead to new paths and fresh mysteries.

And it keeps on going. As Freshly Baked Games gradually peels back Neasied's layers, it constantly finds new ways to awe: yet more surprises, yet more secrets, your exploratory digressions earning still more intriguing rewards. There comes a point where you think surely you must have seen everything, explored its deepest caverns and most mysterious tombs, but down you go again. And somewhere around the midpoint, Queen's Domain takes a truly audacious turn. There is a - let's call it a tonal lurch for fear of spoilers - that signals another shift. Suddenly, there are new toys to play with, an autumnal landscape so complex in its demands, it genuinely made my brain pulse. And then it's over. Until it's not.

Retro resolution toggle (reduces visual shimmering). Individual volume sliders for master, volume, and effects; headphones mode audio toggle. Screen shake toggle; tap to run toggle. Mouse look sensitivity and acceleration sliders; invert mouse horizontal and vertical look toggles; configurable keyboard and mouse bindings; invert gamepad horizontal and vertical look toggles; vibration intensity slider; stick swap toggle; stick deadzone adjustment slider; configurable gamepad bindings.

There are, it turns out, secrets beneath the secrets; a still more intricate web of interconnected threads spun throughout this already fascinating, impossibly complex world. The Neasied that welcomes you really is just the tip of it all. Exactly when you'll come to this realisation depends how thorough you are in the earlier hours - how willing you are to explore its darker corners and brave its more punishing realms - but for me the before-and-after managed to divide my 40-ish hour playthrough neatly in two. You reawaken with a sense of a quest unfulfilled, a hint that more is required. And so, in lieu of more explicit direction, you set out again, probe deeper, explore further, and slowly the vaguest shape of a new objective - a succession of tiny threads seemingly all leading in the same direction - begins to emerge. Tucked along uncharted paths, perhaps, or in suspicious corners, or in item descriptions that suddenly feels like clues.

It's here in this second half - something of a dark mirror to the first, in classic Zelda style - that Queen's Domain takes on a more esoteric air; its web of distantly scattered enigmas requiring real dedication to unfurl. Why this pot on a stick? Why this book? How do these unlit lanterns relate to this thing, and that thing, and this other thing on the other side of the world?

It's clever stuff, even if navigating its late-game threads can occasionally feel just a little too much, a little too abstruse, and you'll potentially face a lot of needle-in-a-haystack backtracking if you haven't been taking judicious notes as you go. Do I have any other quibbles? There are perhaps too few boss encounters that feel like genuine events. And while combat generally feels fair and balanced, serving as a propulsive drumbeat to the broader exploratory rhythms, there are a few late-game all-the-enemies-at-once-style difficulty spikes that go just a bit too far. But then another 'What if?' and the world responds, and those frustrations melt away.

I remember a time in my youth - back before dataminers and FAQs - when video games felt unknowable. Endless enigmas built from creativity and code. It felt like anything could be hiding in there if only you knew where to look, and it was easy to let your imagination run wild. Queen's Domain is so full of secrets, so full of mystery and wonder, it feels like the sort of game my enthusiastic child-brain might have dreamed up all those years ago. 40 hours in, I've seen an ending, but it feels like I'm still far from through. So many mysteries still linger, so many things remain on my checklist of speculative to-dos. Out in the real-world, I've got a Zelda and a GTA awaiting. But it says everything about Queen's Domain - this brilliant, vast, deep enigma; this absurdly audacious thing - that I'm still not yet ready to let go.

A copy of Queen's Domain was provided for this review by Future Friends Games.

#10615. Has Rockstar taken the "Grand Theft Auto" out of GTA 6? Or has it just gamified car-jacking properly for the first time?

It's impossible to have a big game launch these days without some section of the internet, somewhere, finding grievances with the final product because it does not align with the expectations they had pre-release.

The most recent upset comes in the form of Grand Theft Auto 6's revised take on, well, grand theft auto. In case you're one of the few still unfamiliar, grand theft auto is more than the titular moniker for Rockstar's premier crime simulation series; it's actually a US legal term for the act of stealing or attempting to steal a motor vehicle. In most previous GTA entries, this was a central part of the core gameplay loop: accept a mission, nick a car, complete the mission, nick another car, getaway. It's an endemic part of the loop, and has become so popular and identifiable as such that even other games - Watch Dogs, Saint's Row, Cyberpunk 2077, and so on - have emulated the mechanic.

So it makes sense that the internet is in a bit of a spin about GTA 6's more mature and restrained take on its eponymous crime. A quick, no-frills getaway in a vehicle you've hijacked on the road isn't always the most effective option. A mindless crime spree on the streets of Leonida is not a simple thing to pull off, nor an easy thing to live down. In this game, actions have consequences, and an ill-timed or ill-judged car jacking can quickly and efficiently put an end to your criminal ambitions.

That's not to say you cannot simply steal cars. Of course you can: the option to saunter up to an occupied vehicle and jack it from its registered owner is available to you from the very beginning of the game. But the citizens of Leonida aren't going to take your wanton act of foolishness lying down; many of the motorists in the county will fight back, pull a piece on you, or run you over mid-robbery attempt. Even if you are lucky to find one of the more pliant residents of the city, you might find something peculiar is happening in their car. I jacked a woman in the city over the weekend, and the cat she was ferrying from place to place decided to attack me, causing me to crash. I've heard other people say they've had muffled cries coming from the boot of the car where someone is transporting a hostage, or incidents where they've jacked the same person multiple times, leading to the poor car's owner to have a breakdown in the middle of the city.

That cat event, the team here at Eurogamer has worked out, is an early game trigger - it seems stealing random cars early in the game will increase the chance of this encounter occurring. Rockstar's guiding hand is more visible here, then, in trying to teach you a lesson: don't steal cars. You're given your own - Jason's two-door coupé utility, a Vapid Creado - that I actually formed quite an emotional bond with in the opening 10-or-so hours of the game. 'Look after this car,' Rockstar insists, tutorialising you how to repair it, customise it, make it all pretty so it's in good condition when you go pick up Lucia for the first time.

Shortly after the 10 hour marker, narrative events whisk you away elsewhere and you're given a 'slim jim', a long, thin tool that allows you to break into locked cars - something you could only do with a quick elbow jab before. The benefit here is two-fold; you can break into cars without setting off their alarms, and without breaking glass and lowering their resale value (something quite important in the earlier sections of the game). So, it takes you until Chapter 2 to start being able to more reliably and consistently break into vehicles. Good.

And it'll take even longer to break into keyless vehicles. We're in 2026 after all, and it's heavily implied this is when GTA 6 is also set - it stands to reason that many cars run keyless fobs, and your old-school tools won't cut it if you want to break into a Zorruso, rather than an old Vapid truck. But then, even when you're well-armed and ready to start an illustrious new career as Vice City's most nefarious car thief, there's more to consider. Most modern cars also come with trackers: standard trackers simply prevent you modding cars you don't own (not an issue you'll face very often), but advanced trackers are more problematic.

Boosting a car with an advanced tracker immediately alerts the cops to your location, and they close in on you fast. The only answer to this problem is flooring it, and outspeeding the signal ping the tracker is sending out: you need to drive fast and cleanly in order to stay mobile long enough for the tracker to deactivate, and this often incurs damage that makes selling these cars to fences a less viable option. You can't even sell cars with trackers installed at all to some fences until you're much, much further into the game.

So, long story short, stealing cars is a faff in GTA 6. But I like that. I like that Rockstar has evolved the gameplay formula to more accurately reflect the state of the world in 2026. Modern problems require modern answers, and given that Rockstar has gone above and beyond in its creation of a sandbox simulation of Florida, I feel the immersion would be broken by just being able to saunter up to any car you want and break into it. No, needing a 'slim jim' or a key cloner - or needing to case my victims properly before trying to jack them - makes for a better game as far as I'm concerned. It makes me roleplay Jason and Lucia better, it makes me engage with the game's systems more, it makes me appreciate the cars that I do have whilst I have them.

It also improves the flow of the game and how I interact with missions. I recently played GTA: Vice City, and after most missions were wrapped up, I would simply fling the nearest motorist onto the sidewalk, hop in their car, and drive away into the Malibu-styled sunset. I can't simply do that in GTA 6: I need to think about my getaway options, whether there's heat on my car, and where my next best (and cheapest) ride is going to be if a robbery or raid goes awry. 'Ah,' I think, 'I understand why there's a garage next to that safehouse on the map now.'

In making the act of grand theft auto harder and more awkward, Rockstar has nudged me into playing Grand Theft Auto the game like a more thoughtful criminal. I play it like someone on the run, who wouldn't just be indiscriminately committing wanton acts of felony theft on a whim, just because I didn't want to run four blocks down the road to meet my pals for a drink. It's a gorgeous example of functionality, design, and narrative dovetailing - one of the key reasons this is a five star game. The complaints about the system, to my ear, don't reflect what Rockstar has built here. Plus, if it bothers you that much, you could always try to play GTA 6 like a mindless thug if you really want to - just expect the world to treat you like one, too.

#10613. MIT’s new lidar chip could give self-driving cars a wider view

Lidar technology uses pulses of infrared light to calculate distances and create detailed 3D maps of the surrounding environment. This allows autonomous vehicles to detect objects in their path and respond quickly. However, conventional lidar sensors are often large and costly, and many rely on moving components that can wear out over time. These limitations make the systems difficult to use in a wider range of settings.

MIT researchers have now developed an approach that could lead to smaller, more durable lidar sensors that operate without any moving parts. Their advance centers on a new silicon-photonics chip, a type of semiconductor device that controls light instead of electrical signals.

Existing lidar systems built with silicon-photonics chips usually have a narrow field of view. As a result, they struggle to scan areas located toward the edges of a scene. Previous attempts to expand this viewing range have often introduced extra noise and reduced measurement accuracy.

The MIT team addressed those problems by creating an array of integrated antennas that greatly limits unwanted crosstalk, which occurs when neighboring antennas interfere with one another. The design allows the chip to scan across a broader field of view while producing less noise than other silicon-photonics-based methods.

The advance could support the development of more capable lidar sensors for challenging uses, including autonomous vehicle navigation, aerial mapping, and the monitoring of construction sites.

"The functionality we demonstrated in this work solves a fundamental problem for integrated optical-phased-array technology, enabling future lidar sensors that can achieve significantly higher performance than we could demonstrate previously," says Jelena Notaros, the Robert J. Shillman Career Development Associate Professor of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS) at MIT, a member of the Research Laboratory of Electronics, and senior author of a paper on this innovation.

The study also includes lead author and EECS graduate student Henry Crawford-Eng, along with EECS graduate students Andres Garcia Coleto, Benjamin M. Mazur, Daniel M. DeSantis, and Tal Sneh. The findings were published recently in Nature Communications.

Many traditional lidar systems use a large rotating unit to direct light pulses across a scene. When the light strikes nearby objects, it reflects back toward the sensor. The returning signals provide the information needed to reconstruct a detailed map of the environment.

Silicon-photonics-based lidar works differently. Rather than rotating a mechanical device, it scans a beam of light electronically in several directions with a system known as an integrated optical phased array (OPA).

At the heart of an OPA is a group of integrated antennas. Each antenna contains tiny, regularly spaced variations along its length. These features, known as corrugations, cause light from an input source to scatter upward and out of the photonic chip.

Researchers can control the direction of the outgoing beam by changing the phase of the light sent to each antenna. Adjusting these phases changes the angle at which the array releases light, making it possible to steer the beam without moving any physical components.

Placing the antennas close together creates a serious obstacle. Neighboring antennas can couple with one another, scrambling the light they produce. Engineers have traditionally prevented this interference by increasing the distance between antennas, but wider spacing creates a different set of problems.

When antennas are too far apart, the array produces several copies of the same beam at different angles. The primary beam can only be moved a limited distance before it becomes difficult to distinguish from these additional copies.

"This limits our field of view, so the autonomous vehicle now only knows what is in front of it for a certain angular range," Garcia Coleto explains.

The unwanted beam copies, called grating lobes, can confuse the sensor and generate false detections. They also consume energy that could otherwise be directed into the main beam.

To overcome this tradeoff, the MIT researchers developed antennas with reduced crosstalk that can be positioned close together without strongly coupling.

In a conventional OPA, every antenna has an identical structure and uses the same pattern of corrugations. When these matching antennas are placed close together, they interact very strongly.

The MIT team instead created a repeating set of three antennas with distinct shapes. They changed the width of the antennas as well as the size and placement of the corrugations. Because the antennas have different geometries, each one also has a different propagation coefficient, which describes how light moves through the structure.

"Because the antennas have very different propagation coefficients, when we put them close together, essentially each antenna doesn't 'see' the antenna next to it. Therefore, it won't couple with its neighbor," Garcia Coleto says.

Making Different Antennas Behave the Same Way

Reducing the coupling was only part of the challenge. Although the antennas needed different propagation coefficients, they still had to release light in the same consistent manner.

The team designed the antennas around three essential requirements.

Each antenna had to emit the same amount of light. Every antenna also needed to release its beam at the same angle when receiving the same wavelength. Finally, the angle of emission had to change evenly across the entire array as the beam was steered.

"We have this challenge where we require the antennas to have different geometries to reduce the crosstalk, but we need to simultaneously design the antennas to have the same emission characteristics. While it is possible to engineer this, it is extremely difficult because, typically, when antennas are designed with different geometries, they tend to behave differently," Crawford-Eng says.

The researchers began by developing the basic electromagnetic theory describing how radiative modes couple. They then used this theoretical framework to guide the design and computer simulation of the antennas.

Based on those calculations, the team manufactured an OPA containing the reduced-crosstalk antennas. The antennas were placed much closer together than those in a conventional system, and the completed device was then tested experimentally.

Interference Falls From About 100 Percent to 1 Percent

Under the conditions of the experiment, a typical OPA would have produced coupling of approximately 100 percent. The MIT design lowered that coupling to about 1 percent while still generating one clean and precise beam.

The system accurately steered the beam across a broad field of view without producing any grating lobes. This combination of wide scanning, low interference, and strong beam quality addresses one of the central obstacles facing integrated lidar technology.

The researchers now plan to refine the method so the system can cover an even broader viewing range. They are also studying another possible approach to wide field-of-view performance that emerged while they were developing the underlying theory.

"This work addresses a longstanding challenge in integrated optical phased arrays: simultaneously achieving both a wide field of view, which requires dense antenna spacing, and high beam quality, which requires low crosstalk between neighboring antennas. The authors solve this problem with an elegant antenna design. Their innovation is an important step forward for chip-scale, solid-state beam-steering technology," says Joyce Poon, professor of electrical and computer engineering at the University of Toronto and director of the Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, who was not involved with this work.

The Semiconductor Research Corporation, the National Science Foundation, an MIT MathWorks Fellowship, the U.S. Department of War, and the MIT Rolf G. Locher Endowed Fellowship supported the research, in part.

Some of the work was carried out using MIT.nano facilities.

Materials provided by Massachusetts Institute of Technology. Original written by Adam Zewe. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10612. Find Orphan Pages by Comparing Your Sitemap with an Internal Crawl

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A page can return 200 OK, exist in an XML sitemap, and still be practically invisible to anyone browsing the site.

You do not need a full commercial crawler to find the obvious cases. A small Node.js script can compare two things:

Anything present in the first set but missing from the second deserves investigation.

You now have URLs that the application publishes through its sitemap but that your HTML crawl could not reach.

The page might be intentionally isolated. It might exist for a campaign. Navigation might depend on JavaScript. Your crawler may have missed authentication or pagination.

Should a normal visitor reasonably be able to discover this page from another page on the site?

Google recommends using standard crawlable elements with resolvable href values for links that should be discoverable. Google's guidance on crawlable links

That is also good web architecture independent of SEO.

Internal links should not exist merely to move ranking signals around.

They should help users move between related tasks.

A technical guide might lead to a product page. A product page might link back to the configuration guide. A category page should make important child resources discoverable without requiring a user to know the exact URL.

This is why on-page SEO and internal linking are closely connected to site architecture. Good internal links clarify relationships for both visitors and crawlers.

At a broader level, crawlability is one part of technical SEO and search visibility. A page that nobody can reliably discover is difficult to evaluate regardless of how strong its content may be.

If an important URL appears disconnected because navigation is created only after client-side JavaScript runs, the script has surfaced a real architectural fact.

You can then decide whether that is acceptable.

If your application genuinely requires rendered-DOM crawling, replace the fetch-and-Cheerio layer with Playwright.

Do not make the simple crawler pretend to be something it is not.

The best output from this tool is not an "SEO score."

It is a concrete question for the development team:

This page exists. The sitemap knows about it. Why can't a normal HTML crawl reach it?

#10610. Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 captures the franchise's bloody absurdity like nothing else

Warhammer 40,000 is a universe of two distinct moods. On the one hand, it's a grimdark future of serious, solemn stories where trillions die in galactic wars of attrition; of tragedies and insurmountable threats. But there's also the other side of Warhammer 40,000, where goofy hilarity and gory absurdity lies. Where Orks paint their cars go-faster red, and where chainaxe-wielding Khorne Berserkers indulge in gleeful hyperviolence.

Perhaps the best praise I can give Boltgun 2 - which, like its 2023 predecessor, is developed by Auroch Digital - is that it honours 40k's sillier side wonderfully. It's unashamedly gory, over-the-top, and, most crucially, fun. Its creators understand that after a long day at work, sometimes all you want to do is shred a guy or five.

Like its predecessor, Boltgun 2 adopts the style of a retro first-person shooter. It's a little bit Doom, a little bit Duke Nukem, as you blast through its low-poly environments and its sprite-based enemies, hoovering up - and using up - ammo and health as you go. I jumped in right as I was starting World of Warcraft: Forever. And in contrast to that game's slow burn beginning, Boltgun 2 is a full-throttle roar right from the word go that rarely lets up. Just moments in, you're belting down corridors - either playing as a Space Marine or a Sister of Battle, depending on your mood - and unloading endless rounds into cultists and Chaos dregs.

I should note that while I'm a Warhammer fan, the original Boltgun passed me by, so this sequel has been something of a revelation. It truly revels in its violent delights, indulging in its hunger for spectacle. As your bullets turn lesser goons into raspberry jam, strong animation work and excellent audio design bring its cartoonish brutality to life. And it's paired with some brilliantly over-the-top weaponry too. Take your basic Boltgun: when you pull the trigger, this beefed-up rifle's fist-sized projectiles erupt with a weighty bang. Weaker enemies get minced in their path, and for the Space Marine, this is the first gun.

As a Space Marine, you've also got a devastating cool-down-based rush attack known as the Obliterating Charge. It's handy for repositioning in a pinch, but it also bursts through enemies with the power of a speeding lorry. The Space Marine has his chainsword for slice-and-dicing too, and tougher enemies really require some elbow grease - you really have to mash that melee button - to get through 'em. Is all this the height of video game sophistication? Absolutely not, but it got me grinning, and it's a visceral thrill that still hasn't lost its sheen.

I also love that the two selectable characters aren't just cosmetic swaps. If you opt for the Sister of Battle, she comes with her own distinct weapons and abilities. I adore her Flamer and the close-quarters havoc it can cause; the way fire roars from its nozzle as sparks fly and black smoke spews. And while she doesn't have an Obliterating Charge, she does get a slide move that's a fantastic way to maintain forward-momentum. Both characters have their own quirks and appeal - the Space Marine is slower and heavier, but offers punchier gunplay, while the Sister of Battle is swifter but lighter in combat - and both, I reckon, warrant their own playthroughs.

It'd also be remiss not to mention Boltgun 2's dedicated 'taunt' button. Both characters have their own voice lines and many are context-specific. It's a fun thing to spam mid-fight, or to hit when celebrating moments of especially grisly victory. I remembered hearing you could get some extra ammo or healing items if you threw a taunt at the floating servo skull that flies beside you throughout the game and, yup, it's true. Little details like that, alongside the smaller secrets hidden around each mission, create an FPS that's so far been a real joy to mess around in.

It's occasionally all a bit floaty, perhaps, but the combination of speed and action is a winner. What elevates it even further, though, are those Warhammer 40,000 flourishes. I've been amazed how swiftly levels introduce new enemies, and how lovingly they're depicted here. Basic Black Legion Chaos Space Marines take centre stage early on, but as you progress, new subfactions are introduced with their own unique quirks. Khorne-loving Berserkers thunder toward you at speed for painful melee strikes, while devilish Bloodletters lurch at your blind spots with giant swords in-hand. In a later mission things are flipped, and the melee-focused enemies are replaced by menacing ranged forces. Noise Marines carry giant sonic weapons that shoot from afar and disrupt your movement, all while their own daemon allies leap in.

There's also something to say about the variety of the missions you'll encounter. Often, you're given a choice of paths through the game, each with their own planets and battlegrounds. These can be snow-laden ruins, unruly jungles, or cities under siege, and things get even wilder the further you go into the game. And while it's not the sort of experience that encourages you to stand still, if you do you'll see some striking views.

There's a moment, not too far into Boltgun 2, when you find yourself in the bowels of a Khorne-controlled world. Here, literal rivers of blood flow, and you can't go two steps without someone or something rushing in. So I whip out a shotgun and charge on in, properly exhilarated by the chaos of it all as I blast stray enemies into mist. It takes a lot to stave off fatigue in a game with this sort of relentless action but Auroch manages it here.

Warhammer 40,000 is, I think, the sort of setting you can't do half-heartedly, and Boltgun 2 feels like a total embrace of the series' wilder, more absurd side. So far, around five hours in, it's a damn good FPS, retro or otherwise. You can grab it for just over £20, and it's even on sale as of writing. For that price it's a no-brainer.
